p p -adic Fourier theory in families
Andrew Graham, Pol van Hoften, Sean Howe

Abstract
. We construct Fourier transforms relating functions and distributions on finite height p p -divisible rigid analytic groups and objects in a dual category of ℤ p \mathbb{Z}_{p} -local systems with analyticity conditions. Our Fourier transforms are formulated as isomorphisms of solid Hopf algebras over arbitrary small v-stacks, and generalize earlier constructions of Amice and Schneider–Teitelbaum. We also construct compatible integral Fourier transforms for p p -divisible groups and their dual Tate modules. As an application, we use the Weierstrass ℘ \wp -function to construct a global Eisenstein measure over the p p -adic modular curve, extending previous constructions of Katz over the ordinary locus and at CM points, and show its generic fiber, the global Eisenstein distribution, gives rise to new families of quaternionic modular forms that overconverge from profinite sets in the rigid analytic supersingular locus.



1.
Introduction

For
A
a locally compact abelian group with Pontryagin dual
A^{\vee}=\hom(A,S^{1})
, there is a universal character

\kappa:A\times A^{\vee}\rightarrow S^{1},\;(x,\varphi)\mapsto\varphi(x).

The Fourier transforms of classical functional analysis are isomorphisms between spaces of measures or distributions on
A
and spaces of functions on
A^{\vee}
obtained by integrating
\kappa
over fibers of the projection to
A
. Pontryagin duality is an involution on the category of locally compact abelian groups preserving universal characters, and thus these Fourier transforms are self-dual. They are moreover functorial, exchange multiplication and convolution, and exchange invariant derivations with multiplication by canonical coordinate functions.

In this paper, we construct Fourier transforms with similar properties in
p
-adic geometry. Unlike the classical setting, we do not know a natural category analogous to locally compact abelian groups that is preserved under Pontryagin duality. Instead, we develop a theory similar to the duality between compact groups and their discrete duals: In our case, we consider
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-local systems with an analyticity condition, whose dual character groups are finite height
p
-divisible rigid analytic groups (in the sense of Fargues
[
14
,
15
]
).

We study these dual categories over an arbitrary small
v
-stack, and construct universal characters in this generality. We then show that integration over the universal character defines dual Fourier isomorphisms between solid Hopf algebras of distributions and functions, exchanging invariant derivations with multiplication by coordinate functions. When the base is the spectrum of a non-archimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and the
p
-divisible rigid analytic group is the character group of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, one of our Fourier transforms specializes to Amice’s
[
1
]
description of the dual of the space of locally analytic functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Similarly, we also recover a previous extension of Amice’s results due to Schneider–Teitelbaum
[
41
]
— in independent work, Kings–Sprang
[
37
]
also provide a generalization of
[
41
]
over non-archimedean extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We also prove a compatibility between our Fourier theory and integral Cartier duality, generalizing the compatibility between the Amice transform and Mahler expansions.

As an application of our results, we construct an Eisenstein measure on the Tate module of the universal elliptic curve over the integral
p
-adic modular curve (viewed as a
p
-adic formal scheme). Our measure specializes to Katz’s Eisenstein measure over the ordinary locus, and to a related construction of Katz at supersingular complex multiplication points. Passing to the associated distribution on the rigid analytic generic fiber yields a global Eisenstein distribution. Using this distribution, we recover the overconvergence from the ordinary locus of the classical weight family of
p
-depleted Eisenstein series parameterized by characters of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. By a similar argument we then construct, for
R
any order in a degree two extension
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, a new family of quaternionic Eisenstein series parameterized by
L
-analytic characters of
R^{\times}
, and show that they overconverge from the profinite locus of formal complex multiplication by
R
to open subsets of the supersingular locus of the
p
-adic modular curve.

1.1.
Fourier theory for rigid analytic character groups

We first state our main theorem when the base is the spectrum of a non-archimedean field
K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. To that end, let
K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a non-archimedean extension, let
\overline{K}
be an algebraic closure, and let
C
be the completion of
\overline{K}
. We use condensed
K
-vector spaces (see
[
43
]
) in place of the usual theory of locally convex
K
-vector spaces to handle topologies on function and distribution spaces.

We fix a continuous representation of
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
on a finite free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
module
\Lambda
, a finite dimensional
K
-vector space
V
, and a
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
-equivariant surjection
\gamma:\Lambda_{C}\twoheadrightarrow V_{C}
. One half of our Fourier transforms will concern functions and distributions on
\Lambda
with an analyticity condition determined by
\gamma
. Explicitly, we define
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)
to be the natural condensed
1
1

1

Sending a profinite set
S
to the
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
-equivariant
\gamma
-locally analytic functions from
\Lambda
to
C^{0}(S,C)
.

K
-vector space of functions from
\Lambda
to
C
that are
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
-equivariant and
\gamma
-locally analytic, i.e., locally on
\Lambda
, factor as the composition of
\gamma
with an analytic function on an open ball in
V_{C}
. We define
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)
to be its condensed dual.

On the other side, we consider the rigid analytic group
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{rig}}
over
K
parametrizing
\gamma
-analytic characters of
\Lambda
. This is a
p
-divisible rigid analytic group in the sense of
[
14
]
, and, by a theorem of Fargues, see
[
14
, Corollaire 17]
, every
p
-divisible rigid analytic group over
K
is isomorphic to some
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{rig}}
. We denote the associated diamond over
\operatorname{Spd}K
by
H_{\gamma}
, and we define
\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma})
to be the natural condensed
2
2

2

Sending a profinite set
S
to
\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma}\times S)
, which makes sense as
H_{\gamma}\times S
is a diamond over
\operatorname{Spd}K
. In this case
\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma})
is equal to the condensed set
\underline{\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{rig}})}
associated to
\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{rig}})
with its usual Fréchet topology.

K
-vector space of functions on
H_{\gamma}
. We write
\mathcal{D}(H_{\gamma})
for the condensed dual of
\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma})
.

The condensed
K
-vector spaces
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda),

\mathcal{D}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda),

\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma}),
and
\mathcal{D}(H_{\gamma})
are solid
3
3

3

We recall that the property of being solid is a robust type of completeness in this setting.
, and we show that the group structures on
\Lambda
and
H_{\gamma}
endow them with the structure of solid Hopf
K
-algebras, i.e., Hopf algebras with respect to the solid tensor product
-\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{K}-
. We also show that there is a universal
\gamma
-locally analytic character
\kappa:\Lambda\times H_{\gamma}\to\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{m,\eta}^{\lozenge}
, where
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{m,\eta}
is the rigid generic fiber of the formal multiplicative group
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
(-)^{\lozenge}
denotes the associated diamond. Our first main theorem concerns the Fourier transforms for this data:

Theorem 1
.

Integration against the universal character
\kappa
defines isomorphisms of solid Hopf algebras over
K

\mathcal{D}(H_{\gamma})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)\textrm{ and }\mathcal{D}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma}).

These isomorphisms are functorial in the triple
(\Lambda,V,\gamma)
and naturally dual to each other.

That these transforms respect the Hopf algebra structures is an analog of the exchange of convolution and multiplication of functions for the classical Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}
.

Example 1.1.1
.

Suppose that
L
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
contained in
K
. Take
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}_{L}
with the trivial Galois action, take
V=K
, and let
\gamma:\mathcal{O}_{L}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}C\to C
be the surjection coming from the embedding of
L
in
C
. Then
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)
can be identified with the space of functions
\mathcal{O}_{L}\to K
that are
L
-locally analytic in the sense that they can be written locally on
\mathcal{O}_{L}
as a convergent power series in one variable with coefficients in
K
. In this setting, the second isomorphism in Theorem
1
recovers the
p
-adic Fourier theory of Schneider–Teitelbaum (
[
41
, Theorem 2.3]
), see Proposition
6.6.6
. As in
[
41
, §3]
, the character group
H_{\gamma}
is a twisted form of the generic fiber of a one-dimensional Lubin–Tate formal group for
L
.

Our Fourier transforms also satisfy equivariance properties with respect to actions of
\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}V
and
\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}V^{*}
. To define these actions, we first note that we can identify
\operatorname{Lie}H_{\gamma}
with
V^{*}
, and that there is a natural logarithm map
\log_{H_{\gamma}}:H_{\gamma}\rightarrow\operatorname{Lie}H_{\gamma}=V^{*}
. We equip
\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma})
with the action of
\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}V^{*}
by invariant differential operators, and we equip
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)
with the action of
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}V^{*}
by multiplication by polynomial functions on
V
pulled back along
\gamma
. Similarly, we equip
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)
with the action
\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}V
by invariant differential operators, and we equip
\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma})
with the action of
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}V
by multiplication by polynomial functions on
V^{*}
pulled back along
\log_{H_{\gamma}}
.

Proposition 1.1.2
.

The Fourier transforms of Theorem
1
are equivariant for the natural actions of
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}V^{*}
and
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}V
described above.

Proposition
1.1.2
is analogous to the statement that the classical Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}
exchanges multiplication by the coordinate
x
with differentiation
\frac{d}{dx}
.

1.1.3.

Before explaining the ingredients that go into the proofs of Theorem
1
and Proposition
1.1.2
, we make some remarks.

Remark 1.1.4
.

The space
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)\subseteq C^{0}(\Lambda,C)^{\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)}
always contains the space of locally constant functions
\bigcup_{n}C^{0}(\Lambda/p^{n}\Lambda,C)^{\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)}
. Via pullback along
\gamma
, the space
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)
also contains the polynomial algebra
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}V^{*}
. The
K
-algebra spanned by products of these polynomials and locally constant functions is dense in
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)
.

Remark 1.1.5
.

In Theorem
7.0.2
we establish an integral analog of Theorem
1
for
p
-divisible groups over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}_{K}
(more generally,
\operatorname{Spf}R
for any
p
-adically complete ring
R
) as an immediate consequence of Cartier duality for finite flat groups schemes. The integral and rational results satisfy a natural compatibility, see Theorem
7.2.6
for a precise statement.

Remark 1.1.6
.

When
K
is a
p
-adic field (i.e., discretely valued with perfect residue field), there is an initial/maximal choice of
\gamma
, given by the natural map
\Lambda_{C}\rightarrow\Lambda_{C}/\mathrm{im}\theta
for
\theta
the Sen operator. When
\mathcal{H}
is a
p
-divisible group over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
\Lambda=T_{p}\mathcal{H}^{\vee}(C)
, the Hodge–Tate map
T_{p}\mathcal{H}^{\vee}(C)\otimes C\rightarrow\omega_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}C
can be identified with this initial map and the rigid generic fiber
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}
is the character variety associated with
\Lambda=T_{p}G^{\vee}(C)
with its usual Galois action and
\gamma
the Hodge–Tate map.

Remark 1.1.7
.

We expect to have Fourier transforms for other related objects: For example, for certain spaces of functions on Banach–Colmez spaces of slopes between
0
and
1
. These Banach–Colmez spaces can be realized (typically in many different ways) as the universal covers, in the sense of
[
44
]
, of
p
-divisible rigid analytic groups. The universal cover is an extension of the
p
-divisible rigid analytic group by its Tate module, and thus such a Fourier transform would be intimately related to Theorem
1
; we make a precise conjecture and detail this connection in
[
20
]
. More generally, one hopes to find an analog of the full category of locally compact abelian groups where our Fourier theory can be situated; Juan Esteban Rodriguez Camargo has suggested to look for a natural category of group objects in analytic stacks closed under extension and Cartier duality.

Remark 1.1.8
.

In
[
27
]
, the integral
p
-adic Fourier theory for
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
is used to study the
p
-adic interpolation of Maass–Shimura operators on the space of
p
-adic modular forms. One of our motivations for proving Theorem
1
and Proposition
1.1.2
is to similarly study the
p
-adic interpolation of Maass–Shimura operators on spaces of
p
-adic automorphic forms over the
\mu
-ordinary locus of more general Shimura varieties (as in e.g.
[
12
]
). We hope that this will have applications to the construction of
p
-adic
L
-functions (as in e.g.
[
22
]
).

1.2.
Sketch of proof

We sketch the proof of Theorem
1
: First, the various compatibilities are essentially formal after the Fourier transforms have been constructed. In particular, using the duality, it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(\Lambda)\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{O}(H_{\gamma})
is an isomorphism.

To obtain this isomorphism we first treat the case where the Galois action is trivial, where the argument is essentially that of Schneider–Teiltelbaum
[
41
, §2]
. More precisely, we begin by considering
V=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}K
and
\gamma
the identity map
\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}C\rightarrow\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}C
. In this case the
\gamma
-locally analytic functions are the locally analytic functions in the usual sense and
H_{\gamma}=\Lambda^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{m,\eta}^{\lozenge}
. In particular, the Fourier transform in this setting is a multi-dimensional Amice transform, and thus the result is argued by reduction to the results of
[
1
]
. To deduce the result for arbitrary
V
but still when the Galois action is trivial, we observe that the
\gamma
-locally analytic functions can be cut out among all locally analytic functions as those annihilated by certain invariant vector fields. By the case of the theorem that has already been established, these are matched on the Fourier dual side with multiplication by coordinate functions composed with the logarithm. Thus the
\gamma
-locally analytic functions are a closed subspace and the associated character group
H_{\gamma}
is a Zariski closed subvariety of
\Lambda^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{m,\eta}
. Because we have already established the Fourier transform is an isomorphism on all locally analytic functions, we deduce that it is also an isomorphism on
\gamma
-locally analytic functions via passage to a closed subspace and its dual quotient.

We obtain the general case of Theorem
1
where the Galois action can be non-trivial by descent from the case of trivial Galois action when
K=C
.

1.3.
Fourier transforms in families

One of the key ingredients in Theorem
1
is our
construction
of the Fourier transforms. To make this construction, we need to work relatively to define the universal
\gamma
-locally analytic character of
\Lambda
, which lives over the base
H_{\gamma}
. Moreover, since we want to prove that Fourier transforms are isomorphisms of condensed sets, we also want to allow our base to be, e.g.,
\operatorname{Spd}K\times S
for
S
a profinite set. Because the constructions and proofs already involve working over these kinds of general bases, it is natural to ask whether a version of Theorem
1
itself holds over a more general base.

Our approach is to establish a maximally general version by working over arbitrary small v-stacks, and to then explain how it specializes to more concrete settings like in Theorem
1
. This relative theory is not just a matter of curiosity or a technical ingredient for the proof over a point — it also plays an essential role in our construction of a global Eisenstein distribution over the
p
-adic modular curve.

1.3.1.
Main results

Let
Y
be a small v-sheaf (or small v-stack) over
\operatorname{Spd}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, i.e., a sheaf on the category of affinoid perfectoid spaces over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
equipped with the v-topology. Let
Y_{\mathrm{v}}
be the category of affinoid perfectoid spaces over
Y
with the v-topology together with its sheaf of rings
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. Let
\Lambda
be a locally free
\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
-module over
Y
, let
V
be a locally free sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules and
let
\gamma:\Lambda\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to V
be a surjective morphism. We define the character group
H_{\gamma}
by the following fiber product

(1.3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{Y}(\Lambda,\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{m,\eta,Y}^{\lozenge})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(V,\mathbb{G}_{a,Y}^{\lozenge})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{Y}(\Lambda,\mathbb{G}_{a,Y}^{\lozenge}).}

\lrcorner

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Log}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\ast}}

For
(A,A^{+})
affinoid perfectoid over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\operatorname{Spd}(A,A^{+})\to Y
a map, the base change
H_{\gamma}\times_{Y}\operatorname{Spd}(A,A^{+})
is given by
(H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{rig}})^{\lozenge}\to\operatorname{Spd}(A,A^{+})
for a unique relative
p
-divisible rigid analytic group
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{rig}}\to\operatorname{Spa}(A,A^{+})
in the sense of Fargues
[
15
]
. This latter property defines the notion of a
p-divisible v-group
over
Y
, see Definition
4.2.1
.

1.3.2.

We consider the sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{H_{\gamma}/Y}
of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-algebras given by the pushforward of
\mathcal{O}_{H_{\gamma}}
along the structure map
H_{\gamma}\to Y
. We let
\mathcal{D}_{H_{\gamma}/Y}=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(\mathcal{O}_{H_{\gamma}/Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y})
be the dual. Using the group structure of
H_{\gamma}
, we equip both
\mathcal{O}_{H_{\gamma}/Y}
and
\mathcal{D}_{H_{\gamma}/Y}
with the structure of a (commutative) Hopf algebra for the solid tensor product of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules using Künneth isomorphisms (see Proposition
5.1.1
). On the dual side, we construct a sheaf
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y}
of
\gamma
-locally analytic functions on
\Lambda
, and we also consider its dual
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y}
. We also equip these with the structure of (commutative) solid Hopf
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-algebras using Künneth isomorphisms (see Proposition
5.2.9
), and show that the universal character
\Lambda\times_{Y}H_{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{m,\eta}^{\lozenge}
lies in
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda/Y}^{\gamma-\mathrm{la}}(H_{\gamma})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(\Lambda\times_{Y}H_{\gamma})
.

Theorem 2
.

Integration against the universal character defines isomorphisms of solid Hopf
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-algebras

(1.3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{H_{\gamma}/Y}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y}\text{ and }\mathcal{D}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}_{H_{\gamma}/Y}

which are functorial in
(\Lambda,V,\gamma)
, compatible with base change in
Y
, and naturally dual.

We also prove an equivariance for actions of
\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}V
and
\operatorname{Sym}^{\bullet}V^{\ast}
in this setting that specializes to Proposition
1.1.2
, see Proposition
6.4.2
and Proposition
6.4.4
. We will say more about the proof of Theorem
2
in
§
1.5
.

1.3.3.

If
Y=\operatorname{Spd}(R,R^{+})
comes from a seminormal rigid space over a non-archimedean field
K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
V
is a trivial vector bundle, we use Theorem
2
to deduce an extension of Theorem
1
where the objects are now solid Hopf
R
-algebras, see Corollary
6.3.4
. This works also when
(R,R^{+})
is a diamantine Huber pair (e.g. affinoid perfectoid), see §
3.1.8
. When the character variety
H_{\gamma}
is the base change of a
p
-divisible group
\mathcal{H}
over
R^{+}
, we also establish in Theorem
7.2.6
a compatibility between this result and an integral Fourier theory for
\mathcal{H}
described in Theorem
7.0.2
(this integral Fourier theory is a consequence of Cartier duality for finite flat groups schemes; see the introduction to §
7
for more details).

Remark 1.3.4
.

The integral compatibility has some interesting consequences related to subtle problems in the comparison of norms on completed tensor products and explicit cases of Bhatt–Scholze’s surjectivity result for perfectoidizations of semiperfectoid rings
[
6
, Theorem 7.4]
due to Fresnel–de Mathan
[
16
]
; see Remark
7.2.4
and Corollary
7.2.7
.

We give an application of these results below.

1.4.
The global Eisenstein measure and distribution

Fix
p>3
and
N\geq 3
coprime to
p
. Let
\mathfrak{Y}
be the modular curve over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
of full level
N
, let
\mathcal{E}
be the universal elliptic curve over
\mathfrak{Y}
and let
\omega_{\mathcal{E}}
be the sheaf of relative differentials on
\mathcal{E}/\mathfrak{Y}
.

For each
n
coprime to
p
, we adapt a construction of Katz
[
31
]
by using our integral Fourier transform to turn the Weierstrass
\wp
-function into a weight two modular form
\mathrm{Eis}^{(n)}
on
\mathfrak{Y}
valued in measures on
T_{p}\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
, i.e., a section

\mathrm{Eis}^{(n)}\in H^{0}\left(\mathfrak{Y},\omega_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}}\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}}(\mathcal{O}_{T_{p}\mathcal{E}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{Y}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}})\right).

This measure interpolates the classical Eisenstein series defined on lattices
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{C}
by

G_{k}(\Lambda)=\frac{(-1)^{k}(k-1)!}{2}\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda\backslash\{0\}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{k}}.

Theorem 3

(see Theorem
8.2.1
, Theorem
8.2.3
, and Example
8.2.6
)
.

(1)

Writing
\mathrm{HT}:T_{p}\mathcal{E}^{\vee}\rightarrow\omega_{\mathcal{E}}
for the Hodge–Tate map, for
k\geq 3
we have

\int_{T_{p}\mathcal{E}^{\vee}}\mathrm{HT}^{k-2}d\mathrm{Eis}^{(n)}=2(1-n^{k})G_{k}\in H^{0}(\mathfrak{Y},\omega^{k}_{\mathcal{E}}).

(2)

The Eisenstein measure
\mu^{(n)}
of
[
30
,
32
]
valued in Katz–Serre
p
-adic modular forms is recovered, up to the constant term and a degree shift by
1
in the moments, by evaluating
\mathrm{Eis}^{(n)}
at the canonical differential over the Katz–Igusa formal scheme.

(3)

The one-variable
p
-adic
L
-functions interpolating Bernoulli-Hurwitz numbers of
[
30
,
32
,
31
,
33
]
are recovered by evaluating
\mathrm{Eis}^{(n)}
at
(y^{2}=4x^{3}-4x,\frac{dx}{y})
.

It is well known that the members of Katz’s Eisenstein family overconverge after passing to the rigid generic fiber
\mathfrak{Y}_{\eta}
. On the other hand, our Eisenstein measure
\mathrm{Eis}^{(n)}
induces, after base change to the rigid generic fiber, a global Hodge–Tate analytic Eisenstein distribution

\mathrm{Eis}^{(n)}_{\eta}\in H^{0}\left(\mathfrak{Y}_{\eta},(\omega^{2}\otimes\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{HT}-\mathrm{la}}_{T_{p}\mathcal{E}_{\eta}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{Y}_{\eta}})\right).

Using this Eisenstein distribution, we recover the classical overconvergence (Example
8.3.1
).

By the same method, we also find that the
p
-adic
L
-functions in the
p\equiv 3\mod 4
case of Theorem
3
-(3) overconverge to open neighborhoods in the supersingular locus. More precisely, we obtain a new family of quaternionic Eisenstein series parameterized by
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}
-analytic characters of
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}}^{\times}
, and overconverge its members from the locus of formal CM by
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}}
, a profinite quaternionic double coset in the supersingular locus. In fact, we make these constructions with
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}}
replaced by any order in a quadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
(Example
8.3.2
).

1.5.
Functional analysis and sheaf theory

We now make some comments on the proof of Theorem
2
. The overall strategy of proof is the same as discussed in §
1.2
, and a significant portion of this paper is devoted to building a sheaf-theoretic formalism to handle the reduction to affinoid perfectoids where the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-local system
\Lambda
and
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module
V
are both free (generalizing the descent from
C
in the proof of Theorem
1
).

Remark 1.5.1
.

When discussing
p
-adic functional analysis, we have elected to use the language of condensed mathematics over the more classical language of locally convex spaces. This has several benefits: First, v-sheaves automatically give rise to sheaves of condensed modules, since covers by profinite sets are naturally covers in the v-topology. This means that the topologies on our sheaves are already encoded in the geometry of the situation. Second, the category of condensed (or solid) modules over a ring satisfies many favorable properties which one does not have in the usual category of locally convex topological spaces (e.g., it is a Grothendieck abelian category with a symmetric monoidal structure satisfying Grothendieck’s axioms (AB4), (AB5), (AB3*) and (AB4*)). Many of our proofs exploit this extra structure.

Finally, when working over a non-archimedean field, the standard condensed objects (Banach/Fréchet spaces etc.) which appear in this article often coincide with their classical analogue as considered in
[
42
]
or
[
39
]
for example. See
[
40
]
for the comparison when the field is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and Appendix
A
for part of the comparison over an arbitrary uniform
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-Banach algebra. Thus, the condensed theory can be seen as a natural extension of the classical theory. We note, however, that it is not at all clear whether the classical theory generalizes well to general uniform Banach algebras (although
[
17
]
develops some theory over affinoid algebras), whereas the condensed perspective is well-suited to handle this relative setting.

1.5.2.

We now highlight some important technical results in the paper. For example, we prove that the sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{H/Y},\mathcal{D}_{H/Y},\mathcal{O}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y},\mathcal{D}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y}

are reflexive and that they satisfy a Künneth formula with respect to the solid tensor product of sheaves of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules, see Proposition
5.1.1
and
5.2.9
. Proving these two propositions uses essentially all of the theory developed in Sections
2
and
3
. A crucial ingredient here is the flatness for the solid tensor product of (strongly countably) Fréchet modules for arbitrary uniform
(R,R^{+})
over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, see Proposition
2.4.3
. This proposition relies on input from the theory of solid functional analysis over
\underline{R}
developed by Bosco in
[
7
, Appendix A]
(following Clausen-Scholze).

Along the way, we also show that the internal hom on the v-site is related to the internal hom in condensed modules, see §
6.3.3
. This is what allows us to deduce Theorem
1
from Theorem
2
. To prove this, we establish a “quasi-coherence” property for the sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{H/Y},\mathcal{D}_{H/Y},\mathcal{O}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y},\mathcal{D}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y}
over fiercely v-complete bases (see Definition
3.1.9
) when
V
, the codomain of
\gamma
, is free. See Proposition
5.1.1
-(1) and
5.2.9
-(1) for precise statements. Proving this quasicoherence for (locally strongly countably) Fréchet sheaves such as
\mathcal{O}_{H/Y}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma\mathrm{-la}}_{\Lambda/Y}
makes crucial use of the flatness mentioned above to commute inverse limits and solid tensor products.

1.6.
Leitfaden

In §
2
, we develop the results in
p
-adic functional analysis needed for this article in the language of condensed mathematics. After this, we discuss the theory of sheaves on small v-stacks in §
3
, and explain how one can naturally view these objects as sheaves of condensed modules. In particular, we extend the theory developed in §
2
to the setting of v-sheaves. With these preliminary results at hand, we then introduce and study (families of)
p
-divisible v-groups over small v-stacks in §
4
, and sheaves of functions associated with such v-groups in §
5
(with the key results being Propositions
5.1.1
and
5.2.9
). In §
6
, we construct the Fourier transforms, establish their equivariance properties, and show that they are isomorphisms, see Theorem
6.3.1
and Corollary
6.3.4
. We then discuss our main theorem over a field
K
in classical language in §
6.5
, and compare with
[
41
]
. In §
7
, we construct integral Fourier transforms for
p
-divisible groups in the sense of Tate over
p
-adically complete rings, and prove a compatibility with the rigid analytic Fourier transforms of Corollary
6.3.4
. In §
8
, we apply our results to construct the global Eisenstein measure and distribution, then study its properties. In the appendix, we compare a small part of §
2
with the theory of topological modules over uniform Banach
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebras, and conclude with a discussion of the Amice transform.

2.
Condensed functional analysis

In this section we will develop the theory of solid condensed functional analysis over uniform Banach
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebras, inspired by and mostly following
[
43
]
,
[
40
]
,
[
7
, Appendix A]
. In §
2.1
, we discuss the basic theory of solid modules over
p
-adically complete rings and the relation to topological modules, and in §
2.2
, we introduce certain standard Banach and Smith modules, and compute their duals. In §
2.3
, we discuss the theory of trace-class and compact type morphisms. In §
2.4
, we introduce (strongly countable) Fréchet modules, prove that these are always flat (Proposition
2.4.3
), and use this property to describe their interactions with tensor products and duals (Proposition
2.4.10
and Corollary
2.4.11
).

2.1.
Preliminaries

Fix an uncountable strong limit cardinal
\kappa
and let
\operatorname{Prof}_{\kappa}
denote the category of profinite sets of cardinality at most
\kappa
, see
[
43
, Remark 1.3]
. Recall that the category of
\kappa
-small condensed sets (abelian groups, rings,…) is defined to be the category of sheaves of sets (abelian groups, rings,…) on
\operatorname{Prof}_{\kappa}
with respect to the topology of finite jointly surjective families of maps, see
[
43
, Definition 2.1]
. The category of condensed sets (abelian groups, rings,…) is defined to be the filtered colimit over all
\kappa
as above of the category of
\kappa
-small condensed sets (abelian groups, rings,…), see
[
43
, Definition 2.11]
.

The category of condensed abelian groups is a Grothendieck abelian category satisfying Grothendieck’s axioms (AB4), (AB5), (AB3*) and (AB4*), see
[
43
, Theorem 1.10]
, which moreover admits all (small) limits and colimits.

2.1.1.

If
R
is a
\kappa
-small condensed ring, then a
\kappa
-small
R
-module is a sheaf of
R
-modules on
\operatorname{Prof}_{\kappa}
. Taking the colimit over
\kappa
, we obtain the abelian tensor category
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\mathrm{cond}}
, which is simply the category of
R
-modules in condensed abelian groups. This category is an abelian category admitting all (small) limits and colimits, which moreover satisfies Grothendieck’s axioms (AB4), (AB5), (AB3*) and (AB4*). Indeed, this follows from the case of condensed abelian groups discussed above (since limits and colimits can be computed on the underlying condensed abelian group, and since exactness can be tested on the underlying condensed abelian group). If
R=\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
\operatorname{Mod}_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\mathrm{cond}}
is simply the category of condensed abelian groups, and we will denote it by
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathrm{cond}}
.

2.1.2.

Recall from
[
43
, Definition 5.1]
the notion of a solid abelian group. The full subcategory
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\operatorname{solid}}\subset\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathrm{cond}}
consisting of solid abelian groups is closed under (small) colimits and limits, and extensions, see
[
43
, Theorem 5.8]
. Therefore, it also satisfies Grothendieck’s axioms (AB4), (AB5), (AB3*) and (AB4*). The category
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\operatorname{solid}}
has a symmetric monoidal structure given by the solid tensor product
\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

If
R
is a condensed commutative ring whose underlying abelian group is solid, then we can consider the category
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\operatorname{solid}}\subset\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\mathrm{cond}}
of condensed
R
-modules whose underlying abelian group is solid. This subcategory is closed under (small) colimits and limits, and extensions, see
[
43
, Theorem 5.8]
. Therefore, it also satisfies Grothendieck’s axioms (AB4), (AB5), (AB3*) and (AB4*). The category
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\operatorname{solid}}
has a symmetric monoidal structure given by the solid tensor product
\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{R}
.

2.1.3.

There is a functor from topological spaces (abelian groups, rings,…) to condensed sets (abelian groups, rings,…) sending
V
to the condensed set
\underline{V}
whose value on a
\kappa
-small profinite set
S
is the set
C^{0}(S,V)
of continuous functions from
S
to
V
, see
[
43
, Example 1.5]
. The functor
V\mapsto\underline{V}
is fully faithful when restricted to
V
whose underlying topological space is compactly generated (e.g., metrizable), see
[
43
, Proposition 1.7]
.

The functor
V\mapsto\underline{V}
has a left adjoint given by
M\mapsto M(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}
, which is a certain way to topologize the evaluation of the condensed set
M
on the one-point profinite set
\ast
. By construction, the topological space
M(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}
is compactly generated.

Lemma 2.1.4
.

For
X=\varinjlim_{i}X_{i}
a countably indexed filtered direct limit of discrete topological spaces, the natural map

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{i}\underline{X_{i}}\to\underline{X}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It suffices to show that continuous maps
S\to X
, with
S
a profinite set, factor through
X_{i}
for some
i
. Since
S
is profinite and
X
is discrete, the image of a continuous map is compact hence finite; the lemma follows.
∎

2.1.5.

If
R
is a topological ring, then we will write
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\mathrm{cond}}=\operatorname{Mod}_{\underline{R}}^{\mathrm{cond}}
for the category of condensed
\underline{R}
-modules. If
R
is a topological ring and
M
is a topological
R
-module, then
\underline{M}
is naturally an
\underline{R}
-module.

Lemma 2.1.6
.

Let
R
be a topological ring. If
M,N
are compactly generated
4
4

4

By this we always mean compactly generated as a topological space.
topological
R
-modules, then the natural map

(2.1.2)

\displaystyle\hom_{C^{0},R}(M,N)\to\hom_{\underline{R}}(\underline{M},\underline{N})

is a bijection.

Proof.

As mentioned in §
2.1.3
, the fully faithfulness for compactly generated topological spaces is
[
43
, Proposition 1.7]
. The condition for a morphism
f:M\to N
of topological spaces to be a homomorphism of
R
-modules comes down to the commutativity of the diagrams

(2.1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\times N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\operatorname{Id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Since the functor from topological spaces to condensed sets commutes with products and is faithful, the commutativity of these diagrams can be checked after passing to condensed sets. Thus a continuous map
M\to N
is a homomorphism of
R
-modules if and only if the induced map
\underline{M}\to\underline{N}
is a homomorphism of
\underline{R}
-modules.
∎

2.1.7.

For a condensed ring
R
and condensed
R
-modules
M,N
, we will write
\Hom_{R}(M,N)
for the internal hom in the category of condensed
R
-modules. If
R
is a topological ring and
M
a topological
R
-module, then for a profinite set
S
we will write
M_{S}=C^{0}(S,M)
for the set of continuous functions
f:S\to M
equipped with the compact open topology. If
R
and
M
are compactly generated Hausdorff, then this is a topological
R
-module, because
C^{0}(S,M)
is the internal hom in the category of Hausdorff compactly generated topological spaces, see
[
9
, Theorem 5.14]
.

Lemma 2.1.8
.

Let
R
be a topological ring and
M,N
be topological
R
-modules. If
R,N,M
are compactly generated and Hausdorff, then for a profinite set
S
there is a natural bijection

(2.1.4)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R}}(\underline{M},\underline{N})(S)\to\hom_{C^{0},R}(M,N_{S}).

Proof.

Write
\pi^{-1}
for the functor which takes sheaves on profinite sets and restricts them to the category of profinite sets over
S
. It has a right adjoint
\pi_{\ast}
which takes a sheaf
F
on profinite sets over
S
and sends it to the sheaf
T\mapsto F(S\times T)
. Using the definition and the adjunction we find that

(2.1.5)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R}}(\underline{M},\underline{N})(S)

\displaystyle:=\hom_{\pi^{-1}\underline{R}}(\pi^{-1}\underline{M},\pi^{-1}\underline{N})

(2.1.6)

\displaystyle=\hom_{\underline{R}}(\underline{M},\pi_{\ast}\pi^{-1}\underline{N}).

The sheaf
\pi_{\ast}\pi^{-1}\underline{N}
is given by

(2.1.7)

\displaystyle T\mapsto C^{0}(T\times S,N)

which we claim is naturally isomorphic to
T\mapsto C^{0}(T,C^{0}(S,N))
. This claim follows from the fact that
C^{0}(S,N)
is the internal hom in the category of weakly Hausdorff compactly generated topological spaces, see
[
9
, Theorem 5.14]
(here we are using the fact that
N
is compactly generated Hausdorff); in particular
C^{0}(S,N)
is itself compactly generated. Thus we have identified
\pi_{\ast}\pi^{-1}\underline{N}=\underline{C^{0}(S,N)}
and by Lemma
2.1.6
we see that

(2.1.8)

\displaystyle\hom_{\underline{R}}(\underline{M},\underline{C^{0}(S,N)})

\displaystyle=\hom_{C^{0},R}(M,C^{0}(S,N)).

∎

Lemma 2.1.9
.

Let
N
be a
p
-adically complete and separated topological abelian group with the
p
-adic topology and let
S
be a profinite set. Then the
p
-adic topology on
C^{0}(S,N)
agrees with the compact-open topology.

Proof.

Since
S
is compact Hausdorff, the compact-open topology on
C^{0}(S,N)
is compactly generated. We now use the fact that
C^{0}(S,N)
, with the compact-open topology, is the internal hom in the category of compactly generated and weakly Hausdorff topological spaces, see
[
9
, Theorem 5.14]
. It follows from this that

(2.1.9)

\displaystyle C^{0}(S,N)

\displaystyle=C^{0}(S,\varprojlim_{n}N/p^{n})

(2.1.10)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}C^{0}(S,N/p^{n}),

and thus it suffices to prove that the compact open topology on
C^{0}(S,N/p^{n})
is the discrete topology. Let
f:S\to N/p^{n}
be a continuous map; we will show that
\{f\}\subset C^{0}(S,N/p^{n})
is open. Note that
f
takes finitely many values
r_{1},\cdots,r_{k}
whose preimages give an open cover
K_{1},\cdots,K_{k}
of
S
. Per definition of the compact-open topology, the subsets
U_{i}\subset C^{0}(S,N/p^{n})
of continuous functions
g
taking
K_{i}
to
r_{i}
are open. Now
\{f\}=\cap_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}
, so that
\{f\}
is open.
∎

2.1.10.

Let
R^{+}
be a
p
-adically complete and separated ring, and let
M,N
be
p
-adically complete and separated
R^{+}
-modules whose topology is the
p
-adic topology.

Proposition 2.1.11
.

If
\underline{M}
is flat for the solid tensor product over
\underline{R^{+}}
, and
N
and
R^{+}
are
p
-torsion free, then the condensed
\underline{R^{+}}
-module
\underline{M\widehat{\otimes}_{R^{+}}N}
is isomorphic to the solid tensor product
\underline{M}\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{N}
.

Proof.

For a profinite set
S=\varprojlim_{i}S_{i}
we compute

(2.1.11)

\displaystyle C^{0}(S,M\widehat{\otimes}_{R^{+}}N)

\displaystyle=C^{0}(S,\varprojlim_{n}(M/p^{n}\otimes_{R^{+}/p^{n}}N/p^{n}))

(2.1.12)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\left(C^{0}(S,M/p^{n}\otimes_{R^{+}/p^{n}}N/p^{n})\right)

(2.1.13)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\left(\varinjlim_{i}C^{0}(S_{i},M/p^{n}\otimes_{R^{+}/p^{n}}N/p^{n})\right).

where the last equality follows from
M/p^{n}\otimes_{R^{+}/p^{n}}N/p^{n}
having the discrete topology. Now we use the fact that tensor products commute with finite products and with colimits to see

(2.1.14)

\displaystyle\varprojlim_{n}\left(\varinjlim_{i}C^{0}(S_{i},M/p^{n}\otimes_{R^{+}/p^{n}}N/p^{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\left(\varinjlim_{i}C^{0}(S_{i},M/p^{n})\otimes_{C^{0}(S_{i},R^{+}/p^{n})}C^{0}(S_{i},N/p^{n})\right)

(2.1.15)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\left(C^{0}(S,M/p^{n})\otimes_{C^{0}(S,R^{+}/p^{n})}C^{0}(S,N/p^{n})\right)

(2.1.16)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}(\underline{M/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}}\underline{N/p^{n}})(S).

So we see that the condensed
\underline{R^{+}}
-module
\underline{M\widehat{\otimes}_{R^{+}}N}
is given by the
p
-adic completion of the (non-solid) condensed tensor product, i.e.,

\underline{M\widehat{\otimes}_{R^{+}}N}=\varprojlim_{n}(\underline{M/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}}\underline{N/p^{n}})=\varprojlim_{n}\left((\underline{M}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{N})/p^{n}\right).

Now for the solid tensor product we use the flatness to write

(2.1.17)

\displaystyle\underline{M}\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{N}=\underline{M}\otimes^{\blacksquare,\mathbb{L}}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{N}.

It then follows from
[
8
, Lemma A.3]
, that the right hand side is derived
p
-adically complete.
We can thus compute

(2.1.18)

\displaystyle\underline{M}\otimes^{\blacksquare,\mathbb{L}}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{N}

\displaystyle=R\varprojlim_{n}\left((\underline{M}\otimes^{\blacksquare,\mathbb{L}}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{N})\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\blacksquare,\mathbb{L}}\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}\right)

(2.1.19)

\displaystyle=R\varprojlim_{n}\left(\underline{M}\otimes^{\blacksquare,\mathbb{L}}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N}\right)

(2.1.20)

\displaystyle=R\varprojlim_{n}\left(\underline{M}\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N}\right)

(2.1.21)

\displaystyle=R\varprojlim_{n}\left(\underline{M/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N}\right)

(2.1.22)

\displaystyle=R\varprojlim_{n}\left(\underline{M/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N}\right).

where the third equality uses the flatness of
\underline{M}
. We now claim that
\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N}
is isomorphic to
\underline{N/p^{n}}
since
N
is
p
-torsion free: For this we first note that
\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N}
(this uses the assumption that
R^{+}
is
p
-torsion free). Now
\underline{N}(S)
is torsion free for all
S
because
N
is torsion free, hence
N
is flat for the (non-solid) condensed tensor product, see
[
8
, Lemma A.5]
. We see that

(2.1.23)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\underline{N}

\displaystyle\simeq\underline{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}}\underline{N}

(2.1.24)

\displaystyle=\underline{N/p^{n}}.

This is already solid, and so its derived solidification equals itself (see
[
43
, Theorem 6.2 and the paragraph following it]
), and so we get
\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N}\simeq\underline{N/p^{n}}
. We now continue with our computation:

(2.1.25)

\displaystyle=R\varprojlim_{n}\left(\underline{M/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}}^{\mathbb{L},\blacksquare}\underline{N/p^{n}}\right)

(2.1.26)

\displaystyle=R\varprojlim_{n}\left(\underline{M/p^{n}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{N/p^{n}}\right)

(2.1.27)

\displaystyle=R\varprojlim_{n}\left(\underline{M/p^{n}\otimes_{R^{+}/p^{n}}N/p^{n}}\right)

(2.1.28)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\left(\underline{M/p^{n}\otimes_{R^{+}/p^{n}}N/p^{n}}\right),

where, in the second equality we have used that
\underline{M/p^{n}}
is a flat
\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}
-module, and
p
-adic completeness of
M,N
has been used in the last step. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 2.1.12
.

The assumption that
M
and
N
have the
p
-adic topology is necessary. Indeed, let
R=\mathbb{Z}_{p},N=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[T]]
with the
(p,T)
-adic topology and
M=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\langle X\rangle
(the
p
-adic completion of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[X]
). In this case
M\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}N
is the
(T,p)
-adic completion of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T,X]
while
\underline{M}\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{N}
is the (condensed module associated with the)
p
-adic completion of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[T]][X]
(which is not
T
-adically complete).

2.2.
Standard modules

Let
(R,R^{+})
be a uniform Huber pair over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, so that
R^{+}
is a
p
-adically complete and separated ring. We define for an index set
I
the solid
\underline{R^{+}}
(resp.
\underline{R}
)-modules

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}:=\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}\underline{R^{+}/p^{n}}

(2.2.2)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R}:=(\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}})[\tfrac{1}{p}].

As suggested by the notation, the module
\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R}
does not depend on the choice of
R^{+}
.

The module
\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}
is solid because it is an inverse limit of discrete
\underline{R^{+}}
-modules, which are automatically solid. Inverting
p
is a filtered colimit, and therefore
\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R}
is also solid. We will refer to solid
\underline{R}
-modules isomorphic to
\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R}
as
orthonormalizable Banach

\underline{R}
-modules. For an index set
I
we also consider the solid
\underline{R^{+}}
(resp.
\underline{R}
)-modules

(2.2.3)

\displaystyle\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\;\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}],

where we note as before that the second module does not depend on the choice of
R^{+}
. We will refer to solid
\underline{R}
-modules isomorphic to
(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}})[\tfrac{1}{p}]
as
free Smith

\underline{R}
-modules. When
I
is countable, then we will call these modules
countably orthonormalizable Banach
and
countably free Smith
.

2.2.1.

For an index set
I
, we can also form the topological
R^{+}
-modules

(2.2.4)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I}R^{+}

\displaystyle:=\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}R^{+}/p^{n},\qquad\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+},

with the
p
-adic and product topologies respectively. Since
M\mapsto\underline{M}
commutes with limits and takes direct sums of discrete modules to direct sums, see Lemma
2.1.4
, it follows that the natural maps

(2.2.5)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}

\displaystyle\to\underline{\hat{\oplus}_{I}R^{+}}

(2.2.6)

\displaystyle\underline{\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}}

\displaystyle\to\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}

are isomorphisms. We also have the following lemma (which we do not generally expect to hold for the product when
I
is not countable).

Lemma 2.2.2
.

The topological spaces
\hat{\oplus}_{I}R^{+}
and
\hat{\oplus}_{I}R
are compactly generated and Hausdorff. If
I
is countable, then the topological spaces
\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}
and
(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+})[\tfrac{1}{p}]
are compactly generated and Hausdorff.

Proof.

The topological ring
R^{+}
has the
p
-adic topology, which is metrisable (since
R^{+}
is complete and separated for the
p
-adic topology) hence compactly generated and Hausdorff. The topological abelian group
\hat{\oplus}_{I}R^{+}
has the
p
-adic topology, which is metrisable and therefore compactly generated; it is Hausdorff because
\hat{\oplus}_{I}R^{+}
is
p
-adically separated. It follows that
\hat{\oplus}_{I}R
is compactly generated and Hausdorff as it is a countably direct limit of compactly generated Hausdorff topological spaces under open and closed transition maps. The module
\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}
is a countable product of metrisable (Hausdorff) spaces which is (Hausdorff) metrisable, see
[
23
, Satz 4.4.12 on p. 117]
, and thus compactly generated Hausdorff. It follows similarly to before that
(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+})[\tfrac{1}{p}]
is compactly generated and Hausdorff.
∎

2.2.3.

Recall the completed tensor
\widehat{\otimes}
product of linearly topologized modules over linearly topologized rings (in the sense of
[
48
, Tag 07E7]
), see
[
48
, Tag 0AMU]
.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

If
R^{+}\to B^{+}
is a morphism of
p
-complete rings, then the natural maps

(2.2.7)

\displaystyle(\hat{\oplus}_{I}R^{+})\widehat{\otimes}_{R^{+}}B^{+}

\displaystyle\to\hat{\oplus}_{I}B^{+}

(2.2.8)

\displaystyle(\hat{\oplus}_{I}R^{+})\widehat{\otimes}_{R^{+}}(\hat{\oplus}_{J}R^{+})

\displaystyle\to\hat{\oplus}_{I\times J}R^{+}

are isomorphisms.

Proof.

This follows directly from the definition of the completed tensor product, and the fact that (usual) tensor products commute with direct sums.
∎

2.2.5.

We now study properties of these standard modules.

Lemma 2.2.6
.

If
(R,R^{+})
is a uniform Huber pair, then
\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}
is flat for the solid tensor product.

Proof.

First of all,
\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
is flat for the solid tensor product over
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
because it is torsion free, see
[
8
, Lemma A.5]
. By
[
7
, Lemma A.19]
we have
\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}}\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}=\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
which is therefore flat for the solid tensor product over
\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. By
[
8
, Lemma A.4]
, we can write
\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
as a filtered colimit of objects isomorphic to
\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and it is thus flat for the solid tensor product over
\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. It follows from Proposition
2.1.11
and Lemma
2.2.4
that
\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{R^{+}}=\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}
, which is hence flat for the solid tensor product over
\underline{R^{+}}
.
∎

Proposition 2.2.7
.

Let
(R,R^{+})\to(B,B^{+})
be a morphism of uniform Huber pairs over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. For any index set
I
, the natural maps

\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R}^{+}\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R}^{+}}\underline{B}^{+}\xrightarrow{\sim}\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{B}^{+}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R}\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R}}\underline{B}\xrightarrow{\sim}\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{B}

are isomorphisms. Moreover, for any two index sets
I
and
J
, the natural maps

\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\hat{\oplus}_{J}\underline{R^{+}}\to\hat{\oplus}_{I\times J}\underline{R^{+}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad(\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R})\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R}}(\hat{\oplus}_{J}\underline{R})\to\hat{\oplus}_{I\times J}\underline{R}

are isomorphisms.

Proof.

Because the solid tensor product commutes with inverting
p
, it suffices to prove the first and the third isomorphisms. By Proposition
2.1.11
and Lemma
2.2.6
, these reduce to a computation for the completed tensor product, where it is Lemma
2.2.4
.
∎

2.2.8.

Recall that we write
\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}
and
\Hom_{\underline{R}}
for the internal hom in condensed
\underline{R^{+}}
-modules or condensed
\underline{R}
-modules.

Proposition 2.2.9
.

The natural maps

(2.2.9)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)

\displaystyle\to\textstyle\prod_{i\in I}\underline{R}^{+}

(2.2.10)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R}}\left(\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R},\underline{R}\right)

\displaystyle\to\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R}^{+}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}]

are isomorphisms. If
I
is countable, then the natural maps

(2.2.11)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)

(2.2.12)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R}

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\underline{R}}\left(\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}],\underline{R}\right)

are isomorphisms.

Proof.

For the first claim, we follow the proof of
[
40
, Lemma 3.10]
: We note that

(2.2.13)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}}\left(\oplus_{I}\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n},\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}\right)

(2.2.14)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}

(2.2.15)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}.

The second claim follows from the first claim once we establish that the natural map

(2.2.16)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\to\Hom_{\underline{R}}\left(\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R},\underline{R}\right).

is an isomorphism. Using Lemma
2.1.8
(whose assumptions are satisfied by Lemma
2.2.2
), we can identify this map (on
S
points for a profinite set
S
) with the natural map

(2.2.17)

\displaystyle\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}(\hat{\oplus}_{I}R^{+},R^{+}_{S})[\tfrac{1}{p}]\to\hom_{C^{0},R}(\hat{\oplus}_{I}R,R_{S}).

This map is a bijection since any continuous map of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-Banach spaces is bounded. For the third claim, we consider

(2.2.18)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n},\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}\right).

This receives a natural map from
\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}
,

(2.2.19)

\hat{\oplus}_{I}\underline{R^{+}}=\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}}\left(\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n},\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}\right)\to\varprojlim_{n}\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n},\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}\right),

by taking the natural map termwise in the limit. We claim that this map is an isomorphism. We can check this on
S
-points for profinite sets
S
, and by Lemma
2.1.8
applied to
R^{+}/p^{n}
(whose assumptions hold because
\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}/p^{n}
is compactly generated), we can identify the natural map on
S
-points with

(2.2.20)

\displaystyle\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}\Hom_{C^{0},R^{+}/p^{n}}\left(R^{+}/p^{n},R_{S}^{+}/p^{n}\right)\to\varprojlim_{n}\Hom_{C^{0},R^{+}/p^{n}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}/p^{n},R_{S}^{+}/p^{n}\right).

Now
R_{S}^{+}/p^{n}
has the discrete topology (see Lemma
2.1.9
), and so every continuous
R^{+}/p^{n}
-linear map
\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}/p^{n}\to R_{S}^{+}/p^{n}
has kernel containing
\textstyle\prod_{J}\{0\}\times\textstyle\prod_{I\backslash J}R^{+}/p^{n}
for some finite
J\subseteq I
. This implies that (
2.2.19
) is a bijection and the third claim follows.

For the final claim of the proposition, it suffices to show that the natural map

(2.2.21)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\to\Hom_{\underline{R}}\left(\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}],\underline{R}\right)

is an isomorphism. We can check this on
S
-points for profinite sets
S
, and then using Lemma
2.1.8
(using Lemma
2.2.2
to check its assumptions), we can identify this with

(2.2.22)

\displaystyle\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R,R^{+}_{S}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\to\hom_{C^{0},R}\left(\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}],R_{S}\right).

This map is a bijection because the image of
\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}
under any continuous morphism
(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+})[\tfrac{1}{p}]\to R_{S}
is bounded: Indeed, the preimage of
R^{+}_{S}
intersected with
\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}
is open and contains
0
, thus, by the definition of the product topology, contains a set of the form
(\textstyle\prod_{j\in J}p^{n_{j}}R^{+})\times\textstyle\prod_{I\backslash J}R^{+}
for some
J\subseteq I
finite, but then, taking
n=\mathrm{max}_{j\in J}(n_{j})
, we see
\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}
maps into
p^{-n}R^{+}_{S}
.
∎

2.2.10.

We have the following complement to Propositions
2.2.9
and
2.2.7
.

Proposition 2.2.11
.

Let
(R,R^{+})\to(B,B^{+})
be a morphism of uniform Huber pairs over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. If
I,J
are countable sets, then the natural maps

(2.2.23)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I}B^{+}

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\underline{B^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B^{+}},\underline{B^{+}}\right)

(2.2.24)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I}B

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\underline{B}}\left(\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B},\underline{B}\right)

are isomorphisms. Moreover, the natural maps

(2.2.25)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I\times J}R^{+}

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\blacksquare}\textstyle\prod_{J}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)

(2.2.26)

\displaystyle\hat{\oplus}_{I\times J}R

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\left(\textstyle\prod_{J}\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}],\underline{R}\right)

are isomorphisms.

Proof.

For the first isomorphism, we notice that by the tensor-hom adjunction we have

(2.2.27)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{B^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B^{+}},\underline{B^{+}}\right)=\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{B^{+}}\right).

Let
S
be a profinite set. Then it follows from Lemma
2.1.8
(whose assumptions hold by Lemma
2.2.2
), that we may identify

(2.2.28)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{B^{+}}\right)(S)

\displaystyle=\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+},B^{+}_{S}\right)

(2.2.29)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}/p^{n},B^{+}_{S}/p^{n}\right).

Arguing with the product topology as in the proof of Proposition
2.2.9
, we further identify

(2.2.30)

\displaystyle\varprojlim_{n}\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}/p^{n},B^{+}_{S}/p^{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}\hom_{R^{+}}\left(R^{+}/p^{n},B^{+}_{S}/p^{n}\right)

(2.2.31)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}B^{+}_{S}/p^{n}

(2.2.32)

\displaystyle=(\hat{\oplus}_{I}B^{+})(S).

For the second isomorphism, we consider the commutative diagram (for a profinite set
S
)

(2.2.33)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{B^{+}}\right)(S)[\tfrac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+},B^{+}_{S}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\underline{R}}\left(\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}],\underline{B}\right)(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{C^{0},R}\left(\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}\right)[\tfrac{1}{p}],B_{S}\right).}

By the tensor-hom adjunction and the first claim of the Proposition, it suffices to show that the left vertical arrow is an isomorphism. The top horizontal arrow is an isomorphism by the case handled above, and it follows as in the proof of Proposition
2.2.9
that the right vertical arrow is an isomorphism. The bottom horizontal arrow is an isomorphism by Lemma
2.1.8
(using Lemma
2.2.2
to verify the assumptions), and so we are done.

For the third isomorphism we use Proposition
2.2.9
and the tensor-hom adjunction to write

(2.2.34)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\otimes_{\underline{R^{+}}}^{\blacksquare}\textstyle\prod_{J}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{J}\underline{R^{+}},\underline{R^{+}}\right)\right)

(2.2.35)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\hat{\oplus}_{J}\underline{R^{+}}\right).

Let
S
be a profinite set. Then it follows from Lemma
2.1.8
(whose assumptions hold by Lemma
2.2.2
), that

(2.2.36)

\displaystyle\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\hat{\oplus}_{J}\underline{R^{+}}\right)(S)

\displaystyle=\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+},\left(\hat{\oplus}_{J}R^{+}\right)_{S}\right)

(2.2.37)

\displaystyle=\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+},\hat{\oplus}_{J}R^{+}_{S}\right),

where we used Lemma
2.1.9
and Lemma
2.1.4
in the last step. We further compute

(2.2.38)

\displaystyle\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+},\hat{\oplus}_{J}R^{+}_{S}\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}/p^{n}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+}/p^{n},\oplus_{J}R^{+}_{S}/p^{n}\right)

(2.2.39)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}\hom_{R^{+}/p^{n}}\left(R^{+}/p^{n},\oplus_{J}R^{+}_{S}/p^{n}\right),

where the last step follows by arguing with the product topology as in the proof of Proposition
2.2.9
. Continuing, we get (using Lemma
2.1.4
in the second step)

(2.2.40)

\displaystyle\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}\hom_{R^{+}/p^{n}}\left(R^{+}/p^{n},\oplus_{J}R^{+}_{S}/p^{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I}\oplus_{J}R^{+}_{S}/p^{n}

(2.2.41)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}\oplus_{I\times J}R^{+}_{S}/p^{n}

(2.2.42)

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{\oplus}_{I\times J}\underline{R^{+}}\right)(S).

For the final isomorphism, we consider the commutative diagram (for a fixed profinite set
S
)

(2.2.43)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\underline{R^{+}}}\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}},\hat{\oplus}_{J}\underline{R^{+}}\right)(S)[\tfrac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{C^{0},R^{+}}(\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+},\hat{\oplus}_{J}R^{+}_{S})[\tfrac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\underline{R}}((\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}})[\tfrac{1}{p}],\hat{\oplus}_{J}\underline{R})(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{C^{0},R}\left((\textstyle\prod_{I}R^{+})[\tfrac{1}{p}],\hat{\oplus}_{J}R_{S}\right).}

By the tensor-hom adjunction and the third claim of the proposition, it suffices to show that the left vertical arrow is an isomorphism. The top horizontal arrow is an isomorphism by the case handled above, and it follows as in the proof of Proposition
2.2.9
that the right vertical arrow is an isomorphism. The bottom horizontal arrow is an isomorphism by Lemma
2.1.8
(using Lemma
2.2.2
to verify the assumptions), so we are done. ∎

Remark 2.2.12
.

It is not true in general that the natural map
\left(\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{R^{+}}\right)\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R^{+}}}\underline{B^{+}}\to\textstyle\prod_{I}\underline{B^{+}}
is an isomorphism, see Remark
2.1.12
and
[
2
, Theorem 3.27]
.

2.3.
Compact type and trace-class maps

Let
(R,R^{+})
be a uniform Huber pair over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Recall from
[
7
, Definition A.44]
that a morphism
f:M\to N
of solid
\underline{R}
-modules is said to be
trace-class
if
f
is in the image of the natural map

(2.3.1)

\displaystyle\left(\Hom_{\underline{R}}(M,\underline{R})\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N\right)(\ast)\to\hom_{\underline{R}}(M,N).

More concretely, writing
M^{\ast}=\Hom_{\underline{R}}(M,\underline{R})
, there is a morphism
g:\underline{R}\to M^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N
such that
f
is equal to the composition

(2.3.2)

\displaystyle M=M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{R}\xrightarrow{1\otimes g}M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}\otimes 1}N

where
\mathrm{ev}\colon M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\ast}\to\underline{R}
denotes the natural map (given by evaluation).
The following lemma captures some of the basic properties of trace-class morphisms.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
(R,R^{+})\to(B,B^{+})
be a morphism of uniform Huber pairs over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and let
f:M\to N,f^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\to N^{\prime}
be morphisms of solid
\underline{R}
-modules.

(1)

If
f
is trace-class, then so is
f\otimes 1_{\underline{B}}:M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}\to N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}
.

(2)

If
f
and
f^{\prime}
are trace class, then so is
f\otimes f^{\prime}:M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\prime}\to N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
f
is trace class, then there exists a (not necessarily unique) dotted arrow in the commutative diagram

(2.3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\ast\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\ast\ast},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast\ast}}

making both triangles commute. Moreover, the map
f^{\ast\ast}
is also trace-class.

Proof.

The first statement follows directly from examining the commutative diagram

(2.3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\Hom_{\underline{R}}(M,\underline{R})\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N\right)(\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\underline{R}}(M,N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\Hom_{\underline{B}}(M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B},\underline{B})\otimes_{\underline{B}}^{\blacksquare}N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}\right)(\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\underline{B}}(M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B},N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}).}

The second statement follows directly from the commutative diagram

(2.3.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\prime}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\left(M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\prime}\right)^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\prime}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{{}^{\prime}\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N^{\prime}.}

The third statement follows directly from the commutative diagram

(2.3.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\ast\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\ast\ast\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N^{\ast\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\ast\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\ast\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\ast\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N.}

∎

2.3.2.

Let
(R,R^{+})
be a uniform Huber pair over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Following
[
40
, Definition 3.33]
, we define a morphism
f:M\to N
of solid
\underline{R}
-modules to be
compact
if the induced map

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle N^{\ast}\to M^{\ast}

is trace-class. Compact morphisms are not preserved under base change or tensor products in general: Consider
R=\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle
,
M=N=\hat{\oplus}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, viewed as a module supported at
T=0
, and
f
the identity map — since
M^{*}=N^{*}=0
, the map
f
is trivially compact, but its base change to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is not compact. However, we do have the following result.

Lemma 2.3.3
.

Let
(R,R^{+})\to(B,B^{+})
be a morphism of uniform Huber pairs over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and let
f:M\to N,f^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\to N^{\prime}
be morphisms of solid
\underline{R}
-modules. Suppose that
M,N,M^{\prime},N^{\prime}
are direct summands of countably orthonormalizable
\underline{R}
-modules.

(1)

If
f
is compact, then
f\otimes 1_{\underline{B}}:M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}\to N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}
is compact (as a morphism of solid
\underline{B}
-modules).

(2)

If
f
and
f^{\prime}
are both compact, then
f\otimes f^{\prime}\colon M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}M^{\prime}\to N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N^{\prime}
is compact.

Proof.

For the first statement, we note that it follows from Propositions
2.2.9
and
2.2.11
and the exactness of idempotent projection, that the natural map

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle M^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}\to(M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B})^{\ast}

identifies the target with the double dual of the source. It follows from Lemma
2.3.1
-(1) that
N^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}\to M^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B}
is trace class, and then from Lemma
2.3.1
-(3) that
(N\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B})^{\ast}\to(M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\underline{B})^{\ast}
is trace-class. For the second statement, a similar argument works once we note that

(2.3.9)

\displaystyle M^{\ast}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N^{\ast}\to(M\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N)^{\ast}

identifies the target with the double dual of the source; this follows from Propositions
2.2.9
and
2.2.11
and the exactness of idempotent projection.
∎

2.4.
Fréchet modules and flatness

Let
R
be a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-Banach algebra. Recall that
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\mathrm{cond}}
denotes the category of condensed
\underline{R}
-modules, and that
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\operatorname{solid}}
denotes the subcategory of solid modules (i.e., modules over the analytic ring
(\underline{R},\mathbb{Z})_{\blacksquare}
from
[
7
, Proposition A.29]
, noting that
\underline{R}
is solid). Let
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\operatorname{nuc}}
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{solid}}_{R}
consisting of nuclear
\underline{R}
-modules (as in Definition A.40 of
op.cit.
). Note that
\underline{R}
is a nuclear solid
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra (Corollary A.50 in
op.cit.
).

Here are some properties of nuclear
\underline{R}
-modules.

(1)

\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{nuc}}_{R}
is an abelian category stable under the solid tensor product
\otimes^{\blacksquare}_{\underline{R}}
, finite limits, countable products, and all colimits (see
[
7
, Theorem A.43]
).

(2)

Orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-modules are nuclear and flat for the solid tensor product (
[
7
, Proposition A.55.(iii)]
). Also,
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{nuc}}_{R}
is generated under colimits by orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-modules (
[
7
, Proposition A.55.(ii)]
).
5
5

5

We note that the objects denoted by
\Hom_{A}(A[S],A)
in the statement of
[
7
, Proposition A.55.(ii)]
are orthonormalizable Banach, and conversely any orthonormalizable Banach module is of this form. Indeed, this follows from
[
7
, Lemma A.52]
and
[
7
, Proposition A.55.(i)]
.

(3)

The subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\operatorname{nuc}}
generated under filtered colimits by orthonormalizable Banach
R
-modules is stable under countable products (
[
7
, Proposition A.57]
).

2.4.1.

Consider the following definition.

Definition 2.4.2
.

We say a condensed
\underline{R}
-module
M
is
strongly Fréchet
if it is isomorphic to the limit of a countable inverse system
\{M_{n}\}
where each
M_{n}
is a direct summand of an orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-module, and where the transition maps
M_{n+1}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}\to M_{n}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}
have dense image. We say
M
is
strongly countably Fréchet
if each
M_{n}
is a direct summand of a countably orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-module. We say that
M
is of
compact type
if the transition maps
M_{n+1}\to M_{n}
are compact.

We have the following crucial technical result about strongly Fréchet
\underline{R}
-modules.

Proposition 2.4.3
.

If
M
is a strongly Fréchet
\underline{R}
-module, then
M
is flat for the solid tensor product
\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}
.

Proof.

Let
M\simeq\varprojlim M_{n}
as above. Since the forgetful functor
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}^{\mathrm{cond}}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{cond}}
has a left-adjoint, and exactness of sequences can be checked after composing with this forgetful functor, one has a short exact sequence

0\to M\to\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n}\xrightarrow{s}\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n}\to 0

where the map
s
is the identity minus the transition morphisms. Here we are using
[
7
, Lemma A.37]
with
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
to deduce that the sequence is exact on the right. Now each
M_{n}
is a direct summand of an orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-module
N_{n}
, and it follows that

(2.4.1)

\displaystyle\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n}\subset\textstyle\prod_{n}N_{n}

is also the inclusion of a direct summand (take the product of the idempotents). By properties (1) and (2) above (second paragraph of §
2.4
), the module
\textstyle\prod_{n}N_{n}
is nuclear, and by property (3), it can be written as a filtered colimit of orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-modules. Since the solid tensor product commutes with filtered colimits, the category
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{solid}}_{R}
satisfies (AB5), and orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-modules are flat (Lemma
2.2.6
), we see that
\textstyle\prod_{n}N_{n}
is flat for the solid tensor product. This implies that
\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n}
is flat for the solid tensor product since direct summands of (solidly)
\underline{R}
-flat modules are again (solidly)
\underline{R}
-flat.

Let
N
be a solid
\underline{R}
-module, and let
\operatorname{Tor}_{i}^{R,\blacksquare}(-,N)
denote the
i
-th left derived functor of
-\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N
. Passing to the long exact sequence for the above exact sequence, we see that
\operatorname{Tor}_{i}^{R,\blacksquare}(M,N)
(
i\geq 1
) sits in the exact sequence

\operatorname{Tor}_{i+1}^{R,\blacksquare}(\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n},N)\to\operatorname{Tor}_{i}^{R,\blacksquare}(M,N)\to\operatorname{Tor}_{i}^{R,\blacksquare}(\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n},N).

Now, since
\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n}
is flat for the solid tensor product, both of the outer terms vanish (since
i\geq 1
). This implies that
\operatorname{Tor}_{i}^{R,\blacksquare}(M,N)=0
for all
i\geq 1
and all solid
N
, and hence
M
is flat for the solid tensor product.
∎

2.4.4.

We collect the following lemmas. We call an
\underline{R}
-module
M
a
Banach
module if
M=\underline{M^{+}}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
where
M^{+}
is a
p
-torsion free
p
-adically complete and separated topological
R^{+}
-module with the
p
-adic topology. Note that Banach
R^{+}
-modules are automatically metrizable, hence compactly generated.

Lemma 2.4.5
.

Let
(R,R^{+})
be a uniform Huber pair over
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be direct summands of orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-modules and let
N
be a Banach
\underline{R}
-module. If
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is a morphism of
\underline{R}
-modules whose induced morphism on topological
R
-modules has dense image, then the same is true for

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle f\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\operatorname{Id}_{N}:M_{1}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N\to M_{2}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N.

Proof.

Using Lemma
A.1.7
, it suffices to prove that for
i=1,2
we have

(2.4.3)

\displaystyle\left(M_{i}\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}N\right)(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}\simeq M_{i}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}\widehat{\otimes}_{R}N(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}.

This is true when
M_{i}
is orthonormalizable by Proposition
2.1.11
(because then
M_{i}=M_{i}^{+}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
with
M_{i}^{+}
flat over
\underline{R^{+}}
for the solid tensor product, see Lemma
2.2.6
). The case of direct summands follows from the additivity of the completed projective tensor product.
∎

Lemma 2.4.6
.

Let
M=\varprojlim_{n}M_{n}
be a strongly Fréchet
R
-module with presentation as in Definition
2.4.2
. Then the natural map

(2.4.4)

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{n}\Hom_{\underline{R}}(M_{n},\underline{R})\to\Hom_{\underline{R}}(\varprojlim_{n}M_{n},\underline{R})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It suffices to check this on
S
-points for profinite sets
S
. By the adjunction argument of Lemma
2.1.8
, this comes down to showing that

(2.4.5)

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{n}\hom_{\underline{R}}(M_{n},\underline{R_{S}})\to\hom_{\underline{R}}(M,\underline{R_{S}})

is a bijection. Now
M_{n}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}
is compactly generated and thus
\varprojlim_{n}M_{n}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}
is compactly generated.
6
6

6

Indeed, each
M_{n}
has the
p
-adic topology and is thus metrizable, which shows that
\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n}
is also metrizable. Now use that
\varprojlim_{n}M_{n}\subset\textstyle\prod_{n}M_{n}
is closed and thus metrizable and hence compactly generated.
Since
\underline{\varprojlim_{n}M_{n}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}}=M
, we can use Lemma
2.1.6
to reduce to showing that the natural map

(2.4.6)

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{n}\hom_{C^{0},R}(M_{n}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}},R_{S})\to\hom_{C^{0},R}(M(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}},R_{S})

is a bijection. Note that
M
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-Fréchet space and that
R_{S}
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-Banach space, so that the result follows from the proof of
[
40
, Lemma 3.31]
(here we are using the property that
M(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}\to M_{n}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}
has dense image to conclude that any
f\in\hom_{C^{0},R}(M(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}},R_{S})
factors through a continuous
R
-linear
morphism
M_{n}(\ast)_{\mathrm{top}}\to R_{S}
, for some
n
).
∎

2.4.7.

Let
V=\varprojlim_{m}V_{m}
be a strongly countably Fréchet
\underline{R}
-module written as an inverse limit as in Definition
2.4.2
. Let
\{W_{n}\}
be a countable family of direct summands of orthonormalizable Banach
\underline{R}
-modules. We have the following proposition.

Proposition 2.4.8
.

The natural map

(2.4.7)

\displaystyle V\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}\textstyle\prod_{n}W_{n}\to\textstyle\prod_{n}\left(V\otimes_{\underline{R}}^{\blacksquare}W_{n}\right)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
7
, Proposition A.66]
: As in the proof of Proposition
2.4.3
, we can fit
V
in a short exact sequence

(2.4.8)

\displaystyle 0\to V\to\textstyle\prod_{m}V_{m}\to\textstyle\prod_{m}V_{m}\to 0.

We now consider the commutative diagram

(2.4.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
